Well, I figured it was time to come in because... well, I volunteer at the local art museum. Have you been there? Anyway, what brings me in is that, lately, people at the museum will be talking right to me about, say, the next art walk or scheduling, and I’ll be looking right at them... but then I realize I completely missed the middle part of what they said. Anyway, what brings me in is that, lately, people at the museum will be talking right to me about, say, the next art walk or scheduling, and I’ll be looking right at them... but then I realize I completely missed the middle part of what they said. I have to ask them to run it by me again, which is getting a bit embarrassing. It’s like my brain just takes a little vacation. I think it started happening more around the time I retired, which was... gosh, was it five years ago? No, six. Time really flies when you're not punching a clock, doesn't it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your hearing has gotten worse?\n\nPatient: My hearing? No, I don't think so. I mean, I can hear the birds outside just fine. Actually, I’ve been getting really into birdwatching lately since I have the time. I set up this beautiful cedar feeder out on the back porch... I bought it at the farmer's market from this fellow who makes them by hand out of reclaimed barn wood. It’s got this wonderful rustic look to it. I always admired woodworking, though I never really had the patience for all the exact measuring myself. Anyway, the squirrels figured out how to jump onto the feeder from the oak tree, so I had to buy one of those plastic dome things to keep them off. They're clever little guys, aren't they? \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my hearing. No, no, my ears work perfectly fine. I can hear a squirrel scratching at that wood from clear across the house.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Forgetting recent things? Um, no, not really. I mean, my memory is pretty sharp. I can remember the names of all the different birds that come to the feeder, like the tufted titmouse and the Carolina chickadee. Speaking of chickadees, I read the most fascinating article the other day about how they can lower their body temperature at night to survive the winter. It’s called regulated hypothermia, I think. Nature is just amazing, isn't it? I’ve started keeping a little journal of all the birds I see. I bought this beautiful leather-bound notebook at a little stationery shop downtown. I’ve had that pen for... oh, must be twenty years now. 

But, um, anyway, no, I don't think I'm forgetting things. Just the listening part, really.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's funny you ask about when I was younger. I was actually just looking through some old boxes in the attic last week. I was trying to find this old model train set I had as a boy—an O-gauge Lionel, beautiful piece of machinery. The locomotive was die-cast metal, not like the cheap plastic they use today. I used to spend hours setting up the tracks around the Christmas tree. We had this huge Douglas fir one year, must have touched the ceiling... 

But anyway, in one of those boxes, I found a stack of my old report cards from grade school. My mother kept everything, bless her heart. She had this big cedar chest at the foot of her bed where she stored all our keepsakes. It smelled so wonderful when you opened it. And looking at those report cards, I noticed my teachers were always writing things like, "daydreams in class" or "needs reminders to finish work." I guess I've always been a bit like that. In fact, before I was twelve, my mother used to have to pull up a chair and sit right beside me at the kitchen table just to make sure I actually got my homework done instead of staring out the window at the neighbor's dog. It was a Golden Retriever named Buster, sweetest dog you ever met, used to chase his own tail for hours... 

But, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, losing track of conversations. Yeah, I suppose the daydreaming thing has been with me for a long, long time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Anxious? No, not really. I've always been a pretty relaxed sort of fellow. My wife, now, she's the worrier in the family. She'll get anxious about everything, especially when we used to take the camper out. We had this old Coleman pop-up camper... bought it secondhand from a neighbor down the street. It had that distinct canvas smell, you know? Like a mix of old campfire smoke and just a little bit of mildew, but in a good way, if that makes sense. We took it all the way out to Yellowstone back in... oh, must have been the late nineties. The drive out there was just spectacular. I remember we stopped at this little diner in South Dakota that had the absolute best cherry pie I've ever tasted in my life. The crust was so flaky, it just melted right there in your mouth. I tried asking the waitress for the recipe, but she said the cook guarded it like state secrets. Funny how people get so protective over a pie, isn't it? I mean, it's just butter and flour when you get right down to it. 

Anyway, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was it you asked? Oh, right, if I get worried. No, I don't really get anxious about much of anything these days. Life's too short to fret over the small stuff.

The crust was so flaky, it just melted right there in your mouth. I tried asking the waitress for the recipe, but she said the cook guarded it like state secrets. Funny how people get so protective over a pie, isn't it? I mean, it's just butter and flour when you get right down to it. \n\nAnyway, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was it you asked? Oh, right, if I get worried. No, I don't really get anxious about much of anything these days. Life's too short to fret over the small stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this tendency to lose track of what people are saying caused problems for you at work or in daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"The museum group has had to quietly shift some planning pieces away from me, and I’ve stopped feeling confident leading the art walks the way I used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose it has caused a few bumps here and there. I volunteer down at the local history museum a few days a week. We have this wonderful exhibit right now on the old textile mills that used to be the lifeblood of this town. It's fascinating, really. They have these actual looms from the 1920s, and the intricate gear work on them is just a marvel of engineering. I spent a whole afternoon last month just talking to the curator about how they used to source the cotton. He's a brilliant young man, went to school up in Boston, I think. Or maybe it was Providence. Anyway, he knows everything there is to know about industrial history. We were supposed to be organizing a new display case for some old ledger books, which, by the way, have the most beautiful penmanship you've ever seen. People just don't write in cursive like that anymore. 

But, um, where was I? Oh, right, problems. Well, I've noticed the museum group has had to quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me. I think I was missing some of the details during our committee meetings. And... well, I’ve stopped feeling confident leading the art walks the way I used to. I’d get to talking with someone in the group about a painting, and then I'd completely lose the thread of the tour schedule or forget what the other folks had just asked me. So, I mostly just help out in the archives now. It's quieter back there anyway.

I always admired woodworking, though I never really had the patience for all the exact measuring myself.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you were younger, did you also often lose track of what people were saying in conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about when I was younger. I was actually just looking through some old boxes in the attic last week. I was trying to find this old model train set I had as a boy—an O-gauge Lionel, beautiful piece of machinery. The locomotive was die-cast metal, not like the cheap plastic they use today. I used to spend hours setting up the tracks around the Christmas tree. We had this huge Douglas fir one year, must have touched the ceiling... \n\nBut anyway, in one of those boxes, I found a stack of my old report cards from grade school. My mother kept everything, bless her heart. She had this big cedar chest at the foot of her bed where she stored all our keepsakes. It smelled so wonderful when you opened it. And looking at those report cards, I noticed my teachers were always writing things like, \"daydreams in class\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" I guess I've always been a bit like that. In fact, before I was twelve, my mother used to have to pull up a chair and sit right beside me at the kitchen table just to make sure I actually got my homework done instead of staring out the window at the neighbor's dog. It was a Golden Retriever named Buster, sweetest dog you ever met, used to chase his own tail for hours... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, losing track of conversations. Yeah, I suppose the daydreaming thing has been with me for a long, long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I've always been a pretty relaxed sort of fellow. My wife, now, she's the worrier in the family. She'll get anxious about everything, especially when we used to take the camper out. We had this old Coleman pop-up camper... bought it secondhand from a neighbor down the street. It had that distinct canvas smell, you know? Like a mix of old campfire smoke and just a little bit of mildew, but in a good way, if that makes sense. We took it all the way out to Yellowstone back in... oh, must have been the late nineties. The drive out there was just spectacular. I remember we stopped at this little diner in South Dakota that had the absolute best cherry pie I've ever tasted in my life. The crust was so flaky, it just melted right there in your mouth. I tried asking the waitress for the recipe, but she said the cook guarded it like state secrets. Funny how people get so protective over a pie, isn't it? I mean, it's just butter and flour when you get right down to it. \n\nAnyway, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was it you asked? Oh, right, if I get worried. No, I don't really get anxious about much of anything these days. Life's too short to fret over the small stuff.\n\nDoctor: Has this tendency to lose track of what people are saying caused problems for you at work or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it has caused a few bumps here and there. I volunteer down at the local history museum a few days a week. We have this wonderful exhibit right now on the old textile mills that used to be the lifeblood of this town. It's fascinating, really. They have these actual looms from the 1920s, and the intricate gear work on them is just a marvel of engineering. I spent a whole afternoon last month just talking to the curator about how they used to source the cotton. He's a brilliant young man, went to school up in Boston, I think. Or maybe it was Providence. Anyway, he knows everything there is to know about industrial history. We were supposed to be organizing a new display case for some old ledger books, which, by the way, have the most beautiful penmanship you've ever seen. People just don't write in cursive like that anymore. \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, right, problems. Well, I've noticed the museum group has had to quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me. I think I was missing some of the details during our committee meetings. And... well, I’ve stopped feeling confident leading the art walks the way I used to. I’d get to talking with someone in the group about a painting, and then I'd completely lose the thread of the tour schedule or forget what the other folks had just asked me. So, I mostly just help out in the archives now. It's quieter back there anyway.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen people talk to you, do you ever find yourself hearing the sounds but not being able to make out the words?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hearing the sounds but not the words? No, no, my hearing is perfectly fine. I actually had it checked just a couple of years ago when I went in for a physical. The doctor had this little soundproof booth, reminded me of the old telephone booths we used to have on every corner. You remember those? The ones with the heavy folding glass doors and the thick phone books dangling from a chain. I used to keep a handful of dimes in my pocket just in case I needed to make a call. There was this one time, my car broke down on Route 9, an old Ford Galaxie, 1968, beautiful car but the radiator was always giving me trouble... 

Anyway, I had to walk two miles to find a payphone. And when I finally did, the receiver smelled like stale cigar smoke. But it worked! Called my brother-in-law, Frank, to come give me a tow. Frank was a mechanic, could fix anything with an engine. He had this garage that was just crammed floor to ceiling with parts... 

But, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, hearing the words. No, I hear the words just fine. It's just sometimes my mind is... somewhere else, I suppose. But the hearing itself is sharp as a tack.

Anyway, what brings me in is that, lately, people at the museum will be talking right to me about, say, the next art walk or scheduling, and I’ll be looking right at them... but then I realize I completely missed the middle part of what they said. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
